The daily field guide for digital media

AI Media Scout

2026-11-25 · All platforms · Open-source software

The edition has 84 complete guides and 7 screened discoveries. It completes 20 earlier pending guides and details 5 substantive release updates. Velorn v0.4.3 supplies reference imagery for every named cast member in a shot. Phosphene documents a local named-voice route for two authorized characters. Local photo culling, animation inbetweens and editing from recorded dialogue are covered alongside less common studio workflows. OpenSloper exposes editable costume patterns to a model client, dScribe drafts alt text for human review, and Sound2Hap explores turning short environmental sounds into tactile cues. Code grants were read in full. Models, services, creative inputs and hardware or software hosts have separate requirements. This edition is based on source review and published documentation. No discovered app was installed or run. The expanded plan covered 56 fields, 345/345 repository-query attempts and 21 model tasks. The repository lane recorded 345 successful, 0 partial and 0 failed queries, with 99 bounded. The model lane recorded 0 failed tasks. Metadata from 480 saved repository pages included 19,283 numeric identities. The complete retained backlog spans 124,309 candidates, including pilot history and 1,075 previously screened guides still pending before this edition. Creative relevance and the complete software grant were checked before including any discovery. Unreviewed and excluded records stay in local research. Live primary research yielded 217 complete usable responses from 252 distinct URL attempts, including a non-GitHub source in each of 56 fields. 0 captures reached the size bound and 35 other sources were inaccessible or unusable. The ecosystem checks cover all 8 configured groups and record available findings and gaps. Evidence labels in this edition are 0 Recommended, 0 Ready to try, 85 Quality unverified, 6 Experimental. Independent use remains unverified for 88 entries. The creative result check remains unverified for 90, and model, service or cost requirements remain unverified for 91. Repository searches returned no saved rows for Embodied and tactile interactive art, Multiband art and heritage imaging. Primary web research for these fields is included in the coverage notes. 21 model tasks reached the configured response limit of 50 rows. A model record remains a discovery lead until code and use are verified. The separate watchlist made 68 unsuccessful repository checks. Collection logged 8 enrichment failures. Credential-shaped material in unsealed captures was removed before sealing. The affected source origins and before-and-after hashes remain in private evidence.

Search and review counts

56 creative fields; 345/345 repository queries attempted; 21/21 model-task queries attempted. 21576 distinct source candidates and 981 model leads. 84 detailed profiles and 7 additional screened discoveries. Source gaps: 0 failed repository queries, 0 partial repository queries, 99 bounded repository queries, 0 failed model-task queries and 0 web ecosystem gaps. Raw search candidates include duplicates of known tools, excluded projects and projects awaiting review; they are not verified recommendations.

Profiles are based on current primary documentation. Creative quality and performance claims are attributed to the developers unless hands-on testing is explicitly recorded. Unknown hardware requirements stay unknown. Code, model weights, hosted services, and required proprietary hosts can have different terms.

Images & designInvoke documents image-model workflows served through an editable studio. Split image-to-image inference from the layer or canvas editing interface. Save the source, output and model choice so a later color or composition pass is reproducible. 1 direct source attempts for this field were unavailable, bounded or unusable. Video, animation & filmTomasekLibreAIFrames documents RIFE interpolation, selectable upscalers and scene-cut handling. Frame interpolation and timeline editing solve different stages. Compare generated inbetweens over a hard cut and retain the original timing. Source 1 Source 2 Source 3
Audio, music & voiceFaust’s maintainer catalog describes production DSP integrations, including the timestamp-assisted NyxMix workflow. An audio-to-audio model can transform an existing track while the DSP layer handles routing. Keep processed and dry stems aligned for comparison. Source 1 Source 2
3D, reconstruction & assetsThe Gaussian splatting authors describe optimizing a learned scene representation from multiple camera views. A learned view-synthesis representation and editable parametric solids serve different downstream tasks. Preserve separate scene captures and design solids. Source 1 Source 2
Browser tools & web mediaMediaPipe’s web guide exposes learned landmark inference in the browser. The web hand-landmark guide documents client inference inputs and outputs. The WebGPU specification surface depends on browser and device support. Source 1 Source 2
WebXR, VR & ARLearned depth inference can turn a monoscopic panorama into two views for a stereo headset. The Immersive Web project documents a separate runtime for browser-facing XR. Separate the immersive rendering and interaction interface from the model used to produce or interpret an asset. Verify the actual browser and headset route. 2 direct source attempts for this field were unavailable, bounded or unusable. Source 1 Source 2 Source 3
Computational art & creative codingThe captured MediaPipe documentation provides the point data used by a gesture-controlled patch. Hand landmark coordinates can drive a creative coding sketch. Calibrate input coordinate scaling and hold a recoverable neutral state when tracking drops. Source 1 Source 2
Interactive, immersive & live mediaThe Field 4 example shows a patch using learned speech embeddings. A live patch can map learned speech representations to scene controls. Test the mapping with likely performers and keep an editable scene. Source 1 Source 2
3D printing & generative CADThe 3DCraft site describes model-assisted authoring of parametric objects. FreeCAD documents a separate solid-modeling environment. Review generated parametric dimensions and geometry in a solid modeler before any physical cut or print. Source 1 Source 2
Games & production pipelinesGodot’s current import documentation describes WAV audio and loop controls used after generating content. Generated clips still need looping, trim and import checks inside the game engine. Motion capture & character animationThe captured pose and previs sources describe landmark tracking and editable staging respectively. Select the model for the dialogue language and inspect consonants after baking. 1 direct source attempts for this field were unavailable, bounded or unusable. Source 1 Source 2 Source 3
VFX, compositing & relightingThe cited media papers document separate model-assisted lighting and compositing methods. Lighting-aware compositing and coarse learned geometry require shot-specific checks. Keep separate lighting, matte and source layers for revision. Source 1 Source 2 Source 3

Photogrammetry, scanning & neural renderingINRIA’s published implementation describes learning a scene from a photographed set of views. Vector model output can contain difficult paths or raster layers. Inspect editable curves, fill orientation and text before print export. Source 1 Source 2 Source 3

Embodied and tactile interactive artSound2Hap uses a trained model to turn short environmental sounds into tactile cues and evaluates them under specified conditions. Other accessible authoring tools use separate signal-processing methods. Learned audio and gesture signals can become input to an embodied artwork. Rehearse that mapping and retain explicit fallback controls. 3 direct source attempts for this field were unavailable, bounded or unusable. Source 1 Source 2 Source 3
Editable slides and creative publishingThe document-OCR registry describes structured extraction and SVG defines editable vector assets for authoring. Separate extraction or model-assisted layout decisions from the editable vector publication. Check that text and hierarchy remain revisable. Source 1 Source 2

Research beyond GitHub

  • gitlab · searched: Seven project-search terms returned one page each, with six reaching the 20-row request bound. The complete Skiagrafia software grant and learned segmentation-to-vector workflow were checked directly. Other candidates remained private when the code grant or practical AI scope failed review. Source Source
  • codeberg · searched: Five generic API searches returned HTTP 429, limiting breadth. Direct access provided the full software grant, source overview and release record for Facetracker, a Linux OpenSeeFace wrapper. An already-profiled Whisper talk-subtitle project was also accessible by direct source. Source Source Source Source Source
  • sourcehut · searched: Direct project searches for transcription, audio editing and diffusion returned public results. The diffuser source described a local dependency. No additional usable release with a complete verified software grant was established. Source Source Source
  • packages · searched: Complete MIT and Apache license files were extracted from hash-verified localcaption and OmniVoice distributions. Canonical project records cite the registry as supporting evidence. Registry metadata alone never set eligibility. Source Source
  • creative-plugins · searched: The Blender extension registry documents a speech-to-phoneme and lip-sync route. An After Effects route is also documented for frame processing, with host license terms separate. Source Source
  • project-sites · searched: Project sites document direct local-culling, scene-authoring and Windows inference routes. One candidate site was unavailable. Release and software notices were independently checked before inclusion. Source Source Source
  • research-code · searched: The Ambisonizer paper and released implementation describe neural B-format audio synthesis. A second scene-sound paper had demos and a project page, without a released implementation established in this review. Source Source Source
  • international · searched: Chinese publisher documentation shows local screen-caption inference. Japanese and Chinese workflow sources include editable text, stage-specific models and release-specific exports. The Portuguese workflow describes separate editable timeline and captions. Source Source Source

OpenCaptions

Documented creative workflow · AGPL-3.0-only · first-profile

First complete guide in the library. The publication and documentation dates are not necessarily the project’s launch date.

Docs checked 2026-11-25 · 1 GitHub stars · Release v1.3.4 (2026-11-24) · Documentation review; installation not tested

Quality assessment: Quality unverified

Creative use and the code grant are documented. Check-level gaps remain before a recommendation.

Scope: Caption a short review cut on an iPhone or iPad, then correct names and export captions or a rendered copy.

Evidence checked 2026-11-25; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Open-source software license · verified: Reviewed the complete project software grant and its redistribution conditions. Model, data and service terms are scoped separately. [2]
  • Creative results · documented: The iOS guide documents local Whisper and Parakeet with a mobile editing and rendering route. The web documentation lists subtitle and video exports. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]
  • Reproducible setup · documented: The installation and first-use steps are in the cited documentation. Reproduction of this workflow remains unverified. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]
  • Maintenance and support · unknown: The reviewed sources do not establish workflow-specific support, fixes and unresolved blockers.
  • Independent use · unknown: Independent first-hand use of the exact workflow was not established in this source review.
  • Models, services and costs · documented: AGPL-3.0-only source. Model weights, fonts, contributor agreement, trademark policy and App Store distribution have separate terms. Local inference uses device compute. App Store Pro is a one-time purchase for the specified extended exports and styles. The publisher documents an unlocked source build and an optional paid inference route. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Source and documentation review. No hands-on execution by AI Media Scout. The guide documents first-use downloads and device-dependent model optimization. Check timing near cuts and transcriptions in music or noise.

How it uses AI

A selected speech model creates word-level timings. The editor lets you correct individual words, retime them and apply styling before exporting. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Introduction

A selected speech model creates word-level timings. The editor lets you correct individual words, retime them and apply styling before exporting. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

What it is good for

Caption a short review cut on an iPhone or iPad, then correct names and export captions or a rendered copy. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Demo & examples

The iOS guide documents local Whisper and Parakeet with a mobile editing and rendering route. The web documentation lists subtitle and video exports. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Install

Follow the documented route for the scoped workflow. The steps below are source references. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

  1. Use the official iPhone or iPad listing or follow the project’s Xcode source-build instructions.
  2. Choose a supported speech model and language. Allow the model download before going offline.

First project

A small first project makes the input, model output and handoff easy to inspect. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

  1. Listen through a first pass and fix names or incorrectly split words.
  2. Check the caption layout and retime against the intended cut.
  3. Export a review copy or a subtitle file with the selected route.

Hardware & software

Platform and compute constraints refer to the selected route. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Hardware
The iOS guide lists 217 MB for Whisper Small and 627 MB for Whisper Large v3. Clip-specific RAM, VRAM and working storage minima are undocumented.
Software
The mobile guide specifies iOS 18 or later. Apple Speech requires iOS 26 or later. Whisper and Parakeet download separately. The self-hosted web route uses Docker Compose 2.24 or newer.
Platforms
The publisher documents iPhone, iPad and Mac App Store availability and a separate self-hosted web editor. Capacity varies by device.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Dependencies and creative assets carry their own terms. [2] [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

  • Weights: AGPL-3.0-only source. Model weights, fonts, contributor agreement, trademark policy and App Store distribution have separate terms.
  • Commercial: Project code is governed by AGPL-3.0-only. Confirm selected model, source-media and output restrictions separately.
  • Cost: Local inference uses device compute. App Store Pro is a one-time purchase for the specified extended exports and styles. The publisher documents an unlocked source build and an optional paid inference route.

Why it merits attention

The creative workflow is grounded in the cited documentation. Output quality and compatibility are not hands-on verified. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Limitations

The guide documents first-use downloads and device-dependent model optimization. Check timing near cuts and transcriptions in music or noise. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

How it uses AI

Parakeet or Whisper generates word-timed text. Users select sentences and the editor aligns export cuts with silence and picture boundaries. [1]

Introduction

Parakeet or Whisper generates word-timed text. Users select sentences and the editor aligns export cuts with silence and picture boundaries. [1]

What it is good for

Assemble a talking-head first cut from selected transcript sentences and check captions on the result. [1]

Demo & examples

The command-line and local UI examples describe sentence selection, caption styling and cutting near quiet points and hard picture cuts. [1]

Install

Follow the documented route for the scoped workflow. The steps below are source references. [1]

  1. Use the documented Linux or Windows Python and FFmpeg setup.
  2. Fetch the language-appropriate model and fonts and keep job data on local storage.

First project

A small first project makes the input, model output and handoff easy to inspect. [1]

  1. Analyze a sample clip and review its words and timings.
  2. Select a few sentences and export a captioned take.
  3. Review both sides of each join for lost words, stray frames and timing errors.

Hardware & software

Platform and compute constraints refer to the selected route. [1]

Hardware
The README estimates 0.5 GB for the English model and 1.5 GB for Whisper Medium, plus the environment and space for working copies.
Software
Python and FFmpeg. The documented English route uses Parakeet. Other languages use Whisper through faster-whisper.
Platforms
Linux and Windows installation is documented. macOS availability and accelerator minima are undocumented.

  • Cost: Local compute and downloaded model storage. The scoped source workflow lists no mandatory paid speech provider.

The creative workflow is grounded in the cited documentation. Output quality and compatibility are not hands-on verified. [1]

Compare the automatic cuts against the original clip. Export continuity is source-reviewed only. [1]

Software
A browser with module workers, OffscreenCanvas, IndexedDB and DecompressionStream. WebGPU and WebAssembly have distinct performance requirements.
Platforms
The author tested Chromium 152 on Windows 11. Android, macOS, Firefox and Safari routes are listed with fewer verified tests.

Source and documentation review. No hands-on execution by AI Media Scout. The study tested clips with one salient sound source and a specific device setup. Overlapping sound sources, more actuators and broader audiences still need evidence.

How it uses AI

A trained convolutional model translates short environmental sounds into vibrations. The accompanying study compares sound and vibration pairs with fixed signal-processing approaches. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Introduction

A trained convolutional model translates short environmental sounds into vibrations. The accompanying study compares sound and vibration pairs with fixed signal-processing approaches. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

What it is good for

Prototype a tactile cue for a short sound in an installation or interactive scene, then evaluate it on the target actuator. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Demo & examples

The authors report higher ratings for model-generated vibration on audio matching and haptic experience under their study conditions. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Follow the documented route for the scoped workflow. The steps below are source references. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

  2. Select a test audio clip and confirm the model and dependency terms for your use.

First project

  1. Prepare a short sound and run the documented generation route.
  2. Compare the cue with the original sound and a simpler baseline on the intended actuator.
  3. Check response, timing and comfort before using it in a larger installation.

Platforms
Source inference and a project web tool are documented. Packaged desktop binaries, OS minima and on-headset inference are undocumented.

Scope: Edit Japanese dialogue subtitles in ASS and produce either a soft-subtitle MKV or a hard-subtitled review copy.

  • Open-source software license · verified: Reviewed the complete project software grant and its redistribution conditions. Model, data and service terms are scoped separately. [2]

Source and documentation review. No hands-on execution by AI Media Scout. Keep original ASS text and styles when comparing translated tracks. Release-level model support can vary.

How it uses AI

Local Japanese ASR creates timed dialogue captions with configurable speech backends. Optional translation creates a separate reviewed subtitle track. [1] [3]

Introduction

Local Japanese ASR creates timed dialogue captions with configurable speech backends. Optional translation creates a separate reviewed subtitle track. [1] [3]

What it is good for

Edit Japanese dialogue subtitles in ASS and produce either a soft-subtitle MKV or a hard-subtitled review copy. [1] [3]

Demo & examples

The author documents a Windows subtitle editing workflow with waveform, physical ASS rows and export modes. Recognition quality across footage remains unverified. [1] [3]

Install

Follow the documented route for the scoped workflow. The steps below are source references. [1] [3]

  1. Choose the documented Windows installer or portable ZIP.
  2. Prepare FFmpeg and a selected ASR model, checking first-use download sizes and source.

First project

A small first project makes the input, model output and handoff easy to inspect. [1] [3]

  1. Import a clip with approved Japanese dialogue and choose the local transcription model.
  2. Review generated ASS against the waveform and original audio, then save ASS and export the desired subtitle format.

Hardware & software

Platform and compute constraints refer to the selected route. [1] [3]

Hardware
Minimum RAM, VRAM and disk space for the scoped workflow are undocumented.
Software
CPU worker is packaged. FFmpeg and optional CUDA/model dependencies can be downloaded on request. CTranslate2 and CrispASR routes have distinct model lists.
Platforms
Windows desktop is documented. macOS and Linux releases were unavailable in the reviewed documentation.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Dependencies and creative assets carry their own terms. [2] [1] [3]

  • Weights: Apache-2.0 code. Whisper, Kotoba, Qwen, ReazonSpeech and optional translation providers each retain their own terms.
  • Commercial: Project code is governed by Apache-2.0. Confirm selected model, source-media and output restrictions separately.
  • Cost: Local ASR can run without a paid provider. Choosing an online translation backend changes data handling and cost.

The creative workflow is grounded in the cited documentation. Output quality and compatibility are not hands-on verified. [1] [3]

How it uses AI

The desktop app uses text-region detection, language-specific OCR and learned inpainting. It keeps detected regions editable for manual translation, cleanup and lettering. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Introduction

The desktop app uses text-region detection, language-specific OCR and learned inpainting. It keeps detected regions editable for manual translation, cleanup and lettering. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

What it is good for

Relocate and reletter dialogue in a short comic sequence while retaining control over masks and original artwork. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Demo & examples

The maintainers document an editable six-step page workflow and separate mask and typesetting tools. The release assets identify the packaged Windows route. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Install

Follow the documented route for the scoped workflow. The steps below are source references. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

  1. Open the v1.3.1 release and choose the Windows x64 Electron package for this guide.
  2. Open settings and download the specific OCR and inpainting models for the test language. Pick local translation or enter the settings of an authorized external provider.

First project

A small first project makes the input, model output and handoff easy to inspect. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

  1. Import a page you can edit and review its detected balloon boundaries.
  2. Correct recognition errors and compare glossary terms against the original before cleaning the mask.
  3. Preview vertical or horizontal lettering and export a working copy. Keep the original artwork and editable regions.

Hardware & software

Platform and compute constraints refer to the selected route. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Hardware
Minimum RAM, VRAM and disk space for the scoped workflow are undocumented.
Software
The packaged Windows route uses the Electron app and local Python sidecar. The source build lists Bun 1.4 or newer, Node 22.18 or newer and Python 3.12. Tauri v2 needs Rust.
Platforms
v1.3.1 contains Windows x64 desktop installers. macOS and Linux are documented targets with backend manifests, but that specific release does not list desktop installers for them.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Dependencies and creative assets carry their own terms. [2] [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

  • Weights: MIT app. Japan-oriented Manga OCR ONNX and LaMa are listed separately, with further terms for models, fonts, and bundled dependencies. Local translation model terms depend on the selected model.
  • Commercial: Project code is governed by MIT. Confirm selected model, source-media and output restrictions separately.
  • Cost: Local compute and model downloads are required. Optional hosted OCR and translation providers set their own prices and access conditions.

Why it merits attention

The creative workflow is grounded in the cited documentation. Output quality and compatibility are not hands-on verified. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6]

Facetracker

Documented creative workflow · MIT · first-profile

First complete guide in the library. The publication and documentation dates are not necessarily the project’s launch date.

Docs checked 2026-11-25 · Release 26.8.1 (2026-08-07) · Documentation review; installation not tested

Quality assessment: Quality unverified

Creative use and the code grant are documented. Check-level gaps remain before a recommendation.

Scope: Drive a virtual performer with a Linux webcam while keeping the tracker and avatar renderer as separate components.

Evidence checked 2026-11-25; documentation review does not imply hands-on testing.

  • Open-source software license · verified: Reviewed the complete project software grant and its redistribution conditions. Model, data and service terms are scoped separately. [2]
  • Creative results · documented: The publisher documents a webcam-to-avatar workflow and records using a Raspberry Pi 400 at 640×360 with roughly seven tracking frames per second. That is one test configuration. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]
  • Reproducible setup · documented: The installation and first-use steps are in the cited documentation. Reproduction of this workflow remains unverified. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]
  • Maintenance and support · documented: 26.8.1 adds aarch64 and replaces dependence on a prebuilt tracker by updating the bundled OpenSeeFace source. 26.8 added saved webcam and tracking settings. [3]
  • Independent use · unknown: Independent first-hand use of the exact workflow was not established in this source review.
  • Models, services and costs · documented: MIT wrapper, separately licensed OpenSeeFace engine and model files, and separate terms for the avatar renderer and artwork. The Flatpak is free. Local compute, a webcam and a renderer are needed. A selected renderer or character may have its own costs. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

Source and documentation review. No hands-on execution by AI Media Scout. The published timing is a single maintainer observation. No independent tracker-to-avatar test or generic live-performance latency has been verified for this wrapper.

How it uses AI

A Linux desktop wrapper configures and runs OpenSeeFace facial landmark inference on webcam frames. It sends pose and expression data to the selected avatar application. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

Introduction

A Linux desktop wrapper configures and runs OpenSeeFace facial landmark inference on webcam frames. It sends pose and expression data to the selected avatar application. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

What it is good for

Drive a virtual performer with a Linux webcam while keeping the tracker and avatar renderer as separate components. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

Demo & examples

The publisher documents a webcam-to-avatar workflow and records using a Raspberry Pi 400 at 640×360 with roughly seven tracking frames per second. That is one test configuration. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

Install

Follow the documented route for the scoped workflow. The steps below are source references. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

  1. Open the Facetracker package listing and follow its Flatpak installation route for your Linux architecture.
  2. Open Facetracker, select the webcam and set the receiving address and port to match your avatar renderer.

First project

A small first project makes the input, model output and handoff easy to inspect. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

  1. Start a small on-screen rehearsal and check the selected tracking model.
  2. Compare neutral, head-turn and expressive poses with the visible avatar.
  3. Confirm tracking cadence and recovery after turning away before using it for a take.

Hardware & software

Platform and compute constraints refer to the selected route. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

Hardware
The maintainers list tested webcams and one Raspberry Pi 400 example. The package listing reports a 175 MiB download. Minimum RAM, VRAM and sustained rehearsal frame rate are undocumented.
Software
Linux with a webcam and the packaged GTK/libadwaita interface. The Flatpak manifest builds OpenSeeFace and includes ONNX Runtime. A separate avatar receiver renders the animation.
Platforms
The publisher and Flathub document Linux x86_64 and aarch64. The renderer can have a different OS and its own runtime requirements.

License, model weights & costs

The full software license was reviewed. Dependencies and creative assets carry their own terms. [2] [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

  • Weights: MIT wrapper, separately licensed OpenSeeFace engine and model files, and separate terms for the avatar renderer and artwork.
  • Commercial: Project code is governed by MIT. Confirm selected model, source-media and output restrictions separately.
  • Cost: The Flatpak is free. Local compute, a webcam and a renderer are needed. A selected renderer or character may have its own costs.

Why it merits attention

The creative workflow is grounded in the cited documentation. Output quality and compatibility are not hands-on verified. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]

Limitations

The published timing is a single maintainer observation. No independent tracker-to-avatar test or generic live-performance latency has been verified for this wrapper. [1] [3] [4] [5] [6] [7]
